I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Well, I suppose it’s a bit of a long story, but I'll try to explain how it all started. I've been retired for a while now, and my mornings have just become... well, they've become very strange lately. \n\nI wake up, and the sun is coming through the blinds, just like it always does, and instead of getting up to make my coffee—I usually just have a cup of decaf these days, my stomach really can't handle the regular anymore—I just lie there. And this thought creeps in. It’s not a loud thought, just a quiet sort of... wondering. I start thinking that maybe it would just be easier if I hadn't woken up at all. If I could just slip away in my sleep and not have to do any of it anymore. \n\nIt happens so many mornings now. And I want to be very clear about this right away, because I know how it sounds—I don't have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I really don't. I would never actually take steps to do something like that. But just having that thought, just lying there under the quilt and genuinely wishing I didn't have to wake up... it really frightened me. It scared me enough that I finally picked up the phone to ask for help, because sitting with that feeling all by myself every morning is just getting to be too much, and I didn't know who else I could talk to without worrying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying the things you used to find pleasurable, like hobbies, socializing, or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... that’s actually another thing that's been bothering me. It’s funny you ask that, because I was just talking to my daughter on the phone last week—or maybe it was the week before, time sort of blurs together lately—and I was trying to explain it to her without worrying her too much. You see, I've always been a very active person since I retired. I have my Wednesday book club, and then there's the Friday dance night at the community center, which I've been going to for years. \n\nI still make myself go. I really do. I get dressed, I drive myself over there, I walk through the doors... but once I'm there, it's just not the same. It's the strangest feeling, Doctor. I sit there with my friends, people I've known for ages, and they're laughing and chatting about the book or the music, and I just feel like... like I'm sitting behind a thick pane of glass. I can see them, I can hear them, but I'm not really *with* them. I'm just going through the motions. \n\nAnd it's not just the social things, either. I've always taken such pride in my garden. I have these beautiful hydrangeas that I've nurtured for over a decade, and usually, just going out there with my pruning shears gives me such a sense of peace. Or baking—I love baking for the neighbors. I make this lemon drizzle cake that everyone just raves about. But lately... I look at the garden, and it just seems like a chore. I baked that cake last Tuesday for the new couple next door, and pulling it out of the oven, smelling that lemon... it did absolutely nothing for me. That little lift, that spark of joy I used to get from doing things I love, it's just... gone. It's completely flat. And it's so frustrating because I'm doing all the right things, I'm showing up, but the feeling just won't follow.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been consistently low or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just during those mornings you described?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's the whole day. It's really... well, let me think about how to properly describe it to you. Because after those mornings, you know, once I finally do force myself out from under the covers and put my slippers on—I have these wonderful fleece-lined slippers my daughter bought me last Christmas, they're absolute lifesavers on the cold hardwood floors in my hallway—but once I'm up and actually moving around, I wouldn't say I feel sad or depressed, exactly. \n\nI mean, I go about my day. I'll make my decaf, I'll sit and watch the midday news, maybe tidy up the kitchen or sort through the mail... and during all of those normal, everyday things, I don't feel like crying. I don't feel that heavy, dark cloud that you always hear people talk about on television when they discuss feeling depressed. It's really not like that for me most of the day. Like just this past Thursday, for instance, I spent a good three hours organizing the hall closet. I was sorting through all the heavy winter coats and moving some old photo albums up to the top shelf, and I was just... fine. I was just doing the task. I wasn't weeping or feeling miserable or down in the dumps while I was doing it. \n\nSo, no, I really wouldn't say my mood is low most of the day. It's just those terrible, frightening thoughts in the early morning, and then the rest of the day is just sort of... well, existing, I suppose. Just getting through the hours. Like I mentioned with the gardening and the baking, the joy is gone, certainly, but it hasn't been replaced by a deep sadness during the day. It's just a flatness. So if you're asking if I'm walking around feeling blue from dawn until dusk, no, I haven't noticed that at all. It's really just the mornings that are so difficult to get through, and then the rest of the time I'm just going through the motions of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or that you don’t have the energy to do things you normally do, even if they're simple tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, yes. The tiredness... that is something I have definitely noticed, and it's been so incredibly bothersome. It used to be that I could just pop out and do a whole morning of errands—you know, go to the pharmacy, stop by the grocer on Elm Street because they have the best produce, maybe drop off some dry cleaning—and I wouldn't think twice about it. I'd be back by noon and ready to tackle the rest of the day. But lately, even just the simplest errands are... well, they're just exhausting.\n\nFor example, I help out with some administrative things over at the local Cultural Center. I've always enjoyed volunteering there, and we have this upcoming historical exhibit. All I needed to do this past week was get some papers together for the committee. Just organizing a few folders and making sure the dates lined up, really. Nothing strenuous at all. I sat at my dining room table to do it, thinking it would take maybe twenty minutes. But even just doing that... just sorting those papers, or trying to run down to the post office to mail a birthday card... it leaves me feeling completely worn out. \n\nIt's the strangest physical sensation, Doctor. It’s not just being a little sleepy or needing a quick catnap in the afternoon. It honestly feels like my body is moving through thick mud. I was trying to explain it to my neighbor, Martha, when we were at the mailboxes the other day. I said to her, you know when you try to walk in the shallow end of a swimming pool and the water just pushes back so heavily against your legs? It’s exactly like that, but I’m just walking from my kitchen to my hallway. Every movement feels so heavy and deliberate. And it makes getting those simple tasks done feel like I've just run an absolute marathon. I used to have so much energy for the Cultural Center and all my little errands, and now I just sit there feeling like I'm wading through a swamp just to get a few documents in order. It really, truly takes it out of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in absolutely all activities, not just the ones you mentioned earlier, but even in things that used to bring you even small amounts of joy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that’s exactly what I was trying to get at earlier. It really is *everything*, big or small. Like I was saying about the Wednesday book club and the Friday dance nights—I still make myself show up, I really do try. I put on a nice blouse, I drive myself over to the community center, but I just sit there feeling like I'm trapped behind that pane of glass I mentioned. I can see everyone enjoying themselves, but I can't feel it with them. \n\nAnd it’s not just those big social outings where you'd expect to need a lot of enthusiasm. It’s the quiet things, too. The things that used to give me just a tiny, quiet little lift in the middle of the afternoon. I know I brought up my garden and my cooking for the neighbors, but it really is the best example I can give you. I have this beautiful crystal vase that my mother left me, and I used to absolutely love going out with my shears, clipping a few of those big, blue hydrangeas, and arranging them on the kitchen island. Just looking at them would make my whole morning feel brighter. Or baking that lemon drizzle cake—just grating the lemons, the smell of the zest alone used to make me feel so content and purposeful. \n\nBut now, it's just... nothing. I go through the exact same motions, I bake the cakes, I arrange the flowers, I sit with my friends at the center, and I'm just waiting for that little spark of joy to hit me, and it never does. It's completely flat across the board. I truly can't think of a single activity right now that actually brings me any real pleasure, no matter how small or simple it is. It's just so terribly frustrating, Doctor, because I look at my life and I know I *should* be enjoying these things. I've spent my whole retirement building this lovely, active routine, and now it all just feels so incredibly empty, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating much less or much more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because my daughter was actually just commenting on it when she came over on Sunday to help me change a lightbulb in the hallway. She said I was looking a bit thin in the face. And I suppose she's right, though it certainly hasn't been intentional. \n\nI used to really enjoy planning my meals, you know? I'd go down to the grocer, pick out a nice piece of fresh salmon or a good cut of chicken, and make sure I had a proper vegetable and a starch. I've always believed in sitting down to a proper, balanced dinner. But lately... well, food just doesn't call to me much anymore. I’ll open the refrigerator door around noon and just stand there staring at the shelves, and absolutely nothing looks appetizing. Nothing sounds good. \n\nSo I'll just shut the door and think, oh, I'll just fix a little toast. Maybe with just a tiny scrape of butter. Or I'll heat up a small mug of that low-sodium chicken soup I keep in the pantry for when the grandchildren visit. Just something incredibly simple so I don't have to put any effort into it. And I’ll sit at the kitchen island and have a few bites, and then I just sort of forget about it. I'll wander off to sort the mail or tidy up the living room, and then suddenly it's six o'clock, I'm turning on the television for the evening broadcast, and I realize by evening I've barely eaten a single thing all day long. \n\nAnd like I said, it's not on purpose at all. I know a lot of the women my age at the Cultural Center are always trying out some new diet, counting their carbohydrates or skipping meals to drop a dress size, but I'm really not trying to lose weight. I've been the same size for the better part of fifteen years! It's just that my stomach never really rumbles anymore, and the whole ritual of eating just feels like such a monumental chore when you have absolutely zero appetite for it. It's just another thing I seem to have lost my taste for, quite literally, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty, worthless, or like a burden to others lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, the guilt... yes. That’s actually been weighing on me terribly. It’s exactly what keeps just churning in my mind, day in and day out. \n\nIt’s mostly about my sister. I just... I keep going over and over those last weeks with her, playing them back in my head like a broken film reel. I’ll sit there in my armchair in the evenings when the house is quiet, and I just torture myself, really. I keep telling myself that I should have called her more often. I’d think about picking up the phone, you know, maybe just after I finished wiping down the kitchen counters, and then I just wouldn't do it because I felt too tired. Or when we did speak, I feel like I should have listened better, rather than just nodding along or letting my mind wander to my own silly little errands. I just keep thinking I should have done something—anything—more useful for her during that time. \n\nAnd it’s so incredibly frustrating because, well, part of me knows... the rational part of my brain, anyway, knows perfectly well that I couldn't have changed what happened. It was entirely out of my hands, and my daughter constantly reminds me of that. She says, \"Mom, you did what you could,\" but knowing that logically doesn't seem to stop this awful, heavy feeling in my chest. It doesn't stop the thoughts. I just feel so completely worthless when I think about how I handled it, like I was just a disappointment to her when it mattered most. I go round and round with it, just wishing I could go back and be a better sister, even though I know it wouldn't have changed the outcome. It's just a dreadful way to feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't seem to sit still and feel restless much of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think I've noticed anything like that. I mean, I suppose I move a bit slower just because of my age—I'm 69, you know, and my knees certainly aren't what they used to be, especially on rainy days or when it's particularly damp out. I have this little twinge in my right knee that acts up if I've been standing in the kitchen too long, like when I'm trying to bake those cakes I mentioned. But as for moving abnormally slowly, or feeling like I'm moving through molasses, no, I wouldn't say that at all. \n\nAnd as for being restless or unable to sit still... no, quite the opposite, really. I spend most of my time just sitting in my armchair by the front window. It’s a very comfortable chair, floral upholstery, the one I bought when we downsized fifteen years ago. I can sit there for hours, just looking out at the street, watching the neighbor's cat—a big orange tabby—patrol the flowerbeds, or watching the mail carrier come and go. I don't feel any urge to get up and pace or fidget. I just feel so terribly heavy and drained, as I've been saying. \n\nMy daughter did ask me just the other day if I was feeling antsy being cooped up in the house so much, because I used to always be bustling about, tidying this and organizing that. I was the sort of person who couldn't sit through a whole television program without getting up during the commercials to wipe down a counter or fold a load of towels. But now, I just sit. I don't tap my feet, I don't wring my hands, I don't feel like I need to jump out of my skin. I just sit there with all those guilty thoughts about my sister running through my head, but my body itself is perfectly still. Almost too still, if you want to know the truth. It takes a monumental effort just to push myself up out of that chair to go fix that piece of toast we talked about. So no, no restlessness or pacing, and no unusual slowness, just a complete lack of energy to move much at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the time, nearly every day, for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: Two years? Oh, heavens, no. No, nothing like that at all. Two years ago, I was perfectly fine, bustling about, organizing the spring luncheon for the Cultural Center and keeping up with all my usual activities. I was always the one hosting the family dinners, you know, making sure everything was just so. \n\nNo, this whole... this heavy, dreadful feeling we've been talking about, it's really only been going on for about a month. It started right after my sister passed away. And it was just so incredibly unexpected, you see. It wasn't as if she had been ill for a long time and we were prepared for it, where you have time to make your peace with things. It was just a sudden, terrible shock. One week we were on the phone discussing what she was going to bring for the holidays—she always made this wonderful cranberry relish from scratch, a recipe from our mother—and the next, she was just gone. \n\nSo it’s really just been since then, for the past four weeks or so. Ever since we got that awful phone call, I’ve just been in this terrible slump every single day. I wake up in the mornings, and for just a fraction of a second, my mind is clear, and then the reality of it hits me all over again, and that dark cloud just settles right over me for the rest of the day. I'll just sit there in my chair, like I told you, feeling so low and going over everything. But before that happened last month? I was my normal, cheerful self. I really was. It’s just this past month since we lost her that everything has felt so unbearably dark.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I was always the one hosting the family dinners, you know, making sure everything was just so. \n\nNo, this whole... this heavy, dreadful feeling we've been talking about, it's really only been going on for about a month. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose I have been having trouble with things... things that usually wouldn't be a problem at all for me. It's just so hard to focus, to get the energy to do much of anything, really. 

For instance, I'm involved with the Cultural Center, as I think I mentioned, and we're putting together this budgeting workshop for Juneteenth. Usually, I'm the one who handles all the preparation—I love organizing the materials, making sure the spreadsheets are clear, coordinating with the speakers, all of those little details. I've always prided myself on being very thorough. But lately... I just sit there looking at the papers, and my mind just goes completely blank. Or I'll start thinking about my sister again, and before I know it, an hour has gone by and I haven't accomplished a single thing. I actually had to call up Sarah—she's another volunteer, a lovely woman, retired schoolteacher—and ask her to take over a good portion of the prep work. I just couldn't keep up with it the way I normally would. It was terribly embarrassing to admit I couldn't handle it, but I just couldn't.

And it's not just the volunteer work, either. It's my social life, too. I've been turning down invitations left and right. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, asked me over for tea last Tuesday, just a casual thing, and I told her I wasn't feeling up to it. And then my friend Margaret wanted to go to that new garden center that opened up on Elm Street—we usually go every spring to look at the new perennials—and I just couldn't bring myself to go. I just made up some excuse about my knee bothering me. I've been saying no to friends and neighbors much more than usual. I just don't have it in me to put on a cheerful face and make small talk when I feel so dreadful inside. It's just too much effort.

One week we were on the phone discussing what she was going to bring for the holidays—she always made this wonderful cranberry relish from scratch, a recipe from our mother—and the next, she was just gone. \n\nSo it’s really just been since then, for the past four weeks or so. I'll just sit there in my chair, like I told you, feeling so low and going over everything. But before that happened last month? I was my normal, cheerful self. I really was. It’s just this past month since we lost her that everything has felt so unbearably dark.\n\nDoctor: Since your sister passed away a month ago, have you experienced any days where you felt so overwhelmed by sadness that you couldn’t function normally, or had trouble getting through even basic tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have been having trouble with things... things that usually wouldn't be a problem at all for me. Usually, I'm the one who handles all the preparation—I love organizing the materials, making sure the spreadsheets are clear, coordinating with the speakers, all of those little details. I've always prided myself on being very thorough. But lately... I just sit there looking at the papers, and my mind just goes completely blank. Or I'll start thinking about my sister again, and before I know it, an hour has gone by and I haven't accomplished a single thing. I actually had to call up Sarah—she's another volunteer, a lovely woman, retired schoolteacher—and ask her to take over a good portion of the prep work. I just couldn't keep up with it the way I normally would. It was terribly embarrassing to admit I couldn't handle it, but I just couldn't.\n\nAnd it's not just the volunteer work, either. I just made up some excuse about my knee bothering me. I've been saying no to friends and neighbors much more than usual.
